mr president, dear colleagues, i was 10 years old the first time i picked up the phone and a nazi told me that i didn’t belong in sweden, where i was born and raised. he said he would kill me and my family if we didn’t leave the country – my home country. the threats never stopped, but my fear became anger and my anger became political action. i am european. i am elected by the people. a member of this house. and i stand before you as the greens/efa candidate for president of this parliament. to those of you who keep telling me that i don’t belong, you are wrong. the pro-democratic majority of this house will never allow you to define who belongs to this parliament or to our european family. standing here today my thoughts go to our dear colleague, the late president sassoli. he will be remembered for his seemingly effortless commitment to the european project and the work of this house. let us honour his legacy by standing up together for fundamental rights, european solidarity and a european union for all. there are three little words written at the bottom of every resolution, every report and every legislative position in this house. three small words that connect us all: united in diversity. i believe in a european parliament of diversity, but believing in diversity as a strength also means we must defend this european project. the fundamental rights of eu citizens and the rules of democracy are not negotiable. as your president, i will listen to and learn from those who stand up for their rights and our common future. from young people demanding climate justice to minorities demanding a seat at the table and brave europeans risking their own safety to help migrants at our common borders. when governments tell our citizens that they do not have the right to decide over their own bodies, we must stand with them. as your president, i would honour the legacy of the former president simon veil with an initiative to reward individuals and organisations fighting for sexual and reproductive health and rights wherever they are challenged in europe. dear colleagues, we must earn and protect the trust of our citizens by delivering on the biggest challenges that people around europe face today. so let this parliament be a force for social justice, green transition and fair recovery for all. this parliament has declared a climate emergency. let us act on it by ensuring that the commission delivers on the green deal. let’s raise the bar for climate action and the protection of biodiversity, and let’s earn the title as guardian of the treaties, when the commission fails to fulfil this role. this house must lead by example. as your president, i will work tirelessly for a european parliament where diversity is an asset, not a disadvantage. zero tolerance on sexism is possible. let us make anti-harassment training mandatory. let’s create an equal workplace with flexible methods. let’s ensure transparency and internal democracy in this house, ending the time of back-room dealings. let us practice what we preach by making sure our own institution is a front-runner in the green transition. for example, by adding solar panels on the 20 000 square metres of flat roof available on our buildings here in strasbourg. we have had the courage to take on the commission and the council when it was necessary in the past. as your president, i will fight for a feminist parliament that defends and protects. let us be courageous again. mr president, colleagues, before i finish. you cannot be what you cannot see. to all the 10-year-old girls out there who are told that you do not belong, with this candidacy i want to show you that you do. the european parliament, this house, is yours.
madam speaker, mr president, i would like to thank you for your time. i welcome the priorities of the french presidency, but the work of the eu must not stop at the fine print. we must act now. that is a great responsibility on your shoulders, mr. president. the ongoing pandemic requires continued cooperation and coordination to ensure that more people are vaccinated. the eu must take a clear leadership role in the green transition, and this is urgent. our green gift must be gender mainstreamed. one third of women in the eu are unemployed. girls and women are subjected to violence every day, and it is time for an equal europe now. we can't wait for decades. finally, i would like to say that i come from sweden, and that your promise of minimum wages, of ensuring respect for each country's national model, is important for my sweden and for our party model.
executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, if you only knew how i’ve been looking forward to this day. when i was preparing the hearing in this parliament for my second mandate, we discussed what our ambitions should be for the digital services act. and here we are, two years later, because of hard, dedicated, coordinated work in this house. i would very much like to thank the rapporteur, christel schaldemose, for her work and, of course, also the rapporteurs of the associated committees, and the encouragement that it has been for us in the commission to see the interest for so many committees and so many members of this house to make sure that technology serves citizens in our union. shadow rapporteurs, members of your committees, have worked dedicated on this important file and we have come a long way in these 12 months from the tabling of the proposal to the debate that we have today. we also have the general approach unanimously adopted in the council. with today’s debate and the vote that is getting started, i think we are sending a very clear signal to citizens: our democracy has the strength to deal with this; our democracy has the strength to set the rules so that citizens are in charge. it is a unique position that the union has a very strong rulebook that will be enforced fast. this, of course, means further removing barriers to the digital single market, empowering people to make real choices and giving more responsibility to those who provide us digital services. and the guiding principles have already been stated and they remain fundamental: making the internet safer for everyone who’s there, protecting each and every one of us from illegal content, including, of course, unsafe or unauthorised products, while securing the freedom of expression online. this means that it’s not just a slogan to say what is illegal offline should also be illegal online; what is legal offline should also be legal online. it is not just a slogan. with this work that you have done, you have put muscle to the bone. you are making it real. of course, making it real comes with enforcement, and that, of course, comes from the clarity of the regulation, that it can be enforced in a well—resourced, strong system. that requires cooperation in europe, and i think that you as a legislator should expect this, that once we are done with this legislation it will be enforced strongly and fast to the benefit of every citizen and in respect of the legislator.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the house, i would like to say how pleased i am to be here today, in front of you, in front of all of you, to debate a proposal that is very close to my heart, as i believe it is to all of us, and which, as you know, has occupied me since the first day i took office as european commissioner: of course, this famous digital services act. to hold this debate in plenary, a year, almost to the day, barely a day after our 2020 proposal, is a clear indication of the absolutely extraordinary work, i would like to say here, that each and every one of you has done. i would therefore like to begin by expressing my deep gratitude to you and, above all, to mrs christel schaldemose, who has done an absolutely exceptional job of bringing us all together today to have this debate. with this plenary session today and the vote that will follow, we are taking a step which i believe we can call historic and which we all hope will put an end to what we can call the wild west that dominated our information space. just a year ago, as everyone here remembers, we were struck by the images of protesters storming the us capitol on capitol hill, marking, i think, a before and after in the role of digital platforms on our democracy, on our democracies. what we have experienced was probably the culmination of years of unchecked hate speech, incitement to violence, disinformation and destabilisation strategies, spread unchecked on social networks, and from which these networks have, it must be admitted, profited greatly. it became clear to everyone that the absence of rules and controls, including democratic control, over the decisions of a handful of large platforms that have now become systemic public spaces, was no longer tolerable. a few weeks before these incidents, the commission had just presented the dsa and the dma which, in response to the strong expectations of our citizens, proposed a complete reorganisation of our information space. because our citizens expect a strong message from europe to the big platforms that have a growing influence on our lives and on our democracies. we also had the opportunity to hear, for example, the testimony of mrs frances haugen, the digital giant's whistleblower, whom i had the opportunity to meet personally with the dsa rapporteur, christel, and this was highlighted during these talks which confirm that there is not only an urgency to legislate, but also an imperative, and i repeat it here, in front of all of you, not to lower our ambitions. with the dsa, we have created an innovative system that i would like to summarize in four main points. first, we propose clear rules, finally, for the removal of all illegal online products, services or content, with a simple principle, which has been recalled: everything that is prohibited offline must be prohibited online. new obligations are also imposed on very large platforms, based on risk to prevent abuse, as was done with the prudential rules for banks. in addition, the commission will have very significant supervisory and sanctioning powers for these systemic platforms: fines of up to 6% of annual turnover, and even temporary exclusion from the internal market in the event of serious and repeated infringements. we are also opening up, and this is the third point, the black box of algorithms, by imposing transparency measures on platforms to finally understand the reasons why this or that citizen - we have all been the target, often the victim - is targeted by certain content or certain advertisements. finally, we ensure that all these rules are applied uniformly throughout europe, throughout the european union. and of course, these rules will apply to all actors, whether european or not. in just over a year, we have made remarkable progress with the european parliament and the council. i am convinced that we will be able to adopt these proposals together under the french presidency - as the president of the french republic said a few moments ago in this house - by the end of june. the work of the european parliament's committee on the internal market shows that we share the same level of ambition and the same sense of urgency. we will of course remain extremely vigilant together and i welcome the fact that, in this institution too, the increased lobbying efforts we have seen have proved to be in vain. we will not let corporate interests interfere with the general interest of european citizens. europe is the first continent in the world to undertake a comprehensive reform of our digital space. with the dsa and the dma, we are on the verge of reorganizing our digital space in our great internal market, both in terms of the social aspects and also in terms of the economic aspects. a new framework that can perhaps become, without doubt, a benchmark for democracies around the world. today's debate is therefore extremely important for all of us in europe and beyond, and we will therefore be listening to it very carefully.
draft opinion of the consultative committee on culture and education. i am very pleased to see that the commission has taken the initiative to introduce this directive. with the dsa, we have the opportunity to create a milestone in the regulation of online services at union level. this opportunity is used in many points in the present draft, but it is also missed in a number of important points. unfortunately, the opinion of the cult committee was not adequately taken into account in the draft report. the dsa, as it stands, will have serious negative consequences for sectors of the cultural, sports and media sectors. we leave it to the big platforms of our european media to regulate on their terms, if necessary by house rules. media service providers are already highly regulated at both union and national level and are required to adhere to professional editorial standards, regardless of how their content and services are consumed. it is important to protect editorial independence in the media sector. we have already adopted media legislation at european level to deal with cases of disinformation. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. and that is something the commission would have to do if it was to work. media content that is legal offline must be legal online, just as illegal media content online should be illegal offline. making offers to online media is not like buying socks. i would therefore like to ask you to support the amendments tabled by the members of the cult and juri committees. let's take this chance.
draft opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i'm not going to let you down. ladies and gentlemen! i'd like to start by thanking the counter-reporters. through good cooperation with political groups, we have created a draft opinion that improves the situation of women by committing internet service providers to prevent discrimination, combat violence and respond to user signals. we also drew attention to the particular situation of children who are often abused online. we have required isps to develop codes of conduct and to take remedial action. some of our demands were included in the final version of the imco report. we have a long way to go, but we have made progress in protecting children from inappropriate content and women from cyberbullying.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, today the european parliament will send a strong signal that we want a digital market and an internet with clear rules for businesses and safety for users. the internet has made our lives so much better. we can all stay in touch with friends and family, access information, shop online and participate in the public debate, and it will continue to revolutionise the world and humanity in ways we cannot even imagine right now. however, our citizens must also feel safe online. today, we are exposed to illegal content on websites with fraud, disinformation, foreign interference and dangerous products. i’m glad that we – as european lawmakers – have listened to these concerns, and i’m proud that we have been able to unite behind a strong proposal in the digital services act – the dsa. for me, as the negotiator for the largest political force in this house, three things are most important. firstly, that we are now creating a framework to remove illegal content more quickly. this will put an end to the digital wild west, where the biggest platforms are setting the rules and criminal content is going viral. secondly, we want to ensure that the internet remains an open space. the dsa will strengthen the rights for users, increase transparency online and protect freedom of speech. platforms will no longer censor opinions or block accounts without any explanation, and users will be able to contest these decisions taken by the platforms. thirdly, we need a unified digital market where entrepreneurs and start-ups can easily compete, without trade barriers or massive bureaucracy. with the dsa, tech companies in the eu will follow one set of rules, not 27 sets of rules. right now, we are letting the us and china run the show in several areas, and it’s time for europe to take the lead in the global digital race. so let’s make the dsa a worldwide example for a better digital future with safety, openness and innovation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, please! facebook and google have repeatedly shown that they want to make the internet a forum for specially selected perspectives and opinions. as a swedish democrat, i want a free and open arena for thoughts, ideas and exchanges of views. it is the very basis of democracy and development. the proposal we are voting on tomorrow has its points, but unfortunately, on the whole, it does more harm than good. i welcome proposals that promote law enforcement, transparency and consumer protection, but i strongly reject the idea of letting opinion police and private companies decide what constitutes harmful content online. the line should be drawn at illegality, nowhere else. freedom of speech is the foundation of free thought and problem solving. what is controversial today may be taken for granted tomorrow. we need freedom of ideas and opinions, not censorship or central government, not from silicon valley or from brussels.

madam president, i would like to thank you. i'm pretty sure that everyone in this room has, or knows someone who has, fought cancer or is fighting cancer. we still haven't solved the mystery of how we're going to cure cancer. that is why the european parliament's cancer strategy is in so many ways a victory and an important step towards how we can work within the eu to eradicate cancer. the way to do this is through education, research and dedicated work. if we learn from what we've just been through with covid-19, it's very clear that when the eu decides to do something and mobilizes, we can do more. even those who are consistently opposed to eu cooperation should realize this. the fight against cancer doesn't start today, it's been going on for a long time, but today we begin the next powerful step in our collective work to eradicate cancer.
in writing. i am very pleased that the european parliament has taken up such an important issue as the treatment and prevention of cancer in europe. it was on the initiative of the epp group and our polish delegation to the po-psl that a special committee on the fight against cancer was set up in parliament, and we are looking at the results of its work during this plenary session. the report adopted by the committee drew attention to inequalities in the prevention and treatment of cancer in the member states. we don't agree that the chance of survival and the quality of treatment should be determined by the postal code. we need to establish european-wide quality standards for cancer treatment, as well as a review of the existing legal framework for access to cross-border healthcare, including the right to a second opinion. the creation of an eu register of inequalities could also be an important solution, allowing the situation to be monitored and challenges to be diagnosed. it's very important to equalise access to medicines. strengthen the union market for medicines, diversify supply chains and closely monitor any shortages, as well as the supply of strategic stocks of medicines and raw materials. the european medicines agency can play a key role in this. and finally, cancer patients should not be punished for their cancer in their daily lives. we therefore call for the adoption of an anti-discrimination directive and for the fair and equal implementation of directives on financial services such as consumer credit.
in writing. – this is an important report because it aims to strengthen coordination between eu countries to combat inequalities in the fight against cancer. eu countries should work together to combat drug shortages, for example, through joint purchasing procedures. the eu should target investment to effectively develop interoperable electronic health records, digital health solutions for continued cancer out-patient care, as well as multi-sectoral synergies in research and innovation to improve early detection and personalised cancer treatment. this will also be important in achieving the ever important health union. it is important to come up with european prevention and management plans to help national healthcare systems be more resilient and prevent and deal with shortages of drugs, equipment, products, and staff in times of health crisis.
in writing. cancer kills 1.3 million people in europe every year. it's too much. effective, innovative, accessible and affordable treatment is very important. 40% of all cancers are preventable, so all possible prevention measures should be taken. the commission's role is to develop and implement the cancer action plan in the most effective way. the report finds that member states should allow citizens to undergo regular health checks and that healthy lifestyles should be encouraged. there is also a need to continue investing in research into the causes of cancer in adults as well as in children and adolescents, and to organise regular screening studies. equal access to and quality of care remain a problem in many member states and this needs to be addressed by reforming the cross-border healthcare directive to allow mobility and access to highly specialised equipment and treatment.
in writing. mr president, europe is currently facing first and second class citizens in cancer treatment. in eastern europe, cancer patients have a 30 percent lower chance of surviving the disease than do patients in western europe. the fight against and treatment of cancer in the eu must not depend on the social status or geography of the patient. europe must guarantee all its citizens high-quality, accessible and affordable cancer care. this means screening for key cancers, early diagnosis, early treatment and post-treatment rehabilitation. the disparities in cancer treatment must be eliminated and cancer treatment must be made equally accessible throughout europe. the final report by beca shows parliament's ambition in the fight against cancer. the report calls for joint action at eu level. but we should be even more ambitious in our efforts to prevent it. the various chemicals in our environment, poor air quality and micro-particles affect the incidence of cancer. we can't stop the cancer growth prediction unless we solve these problems. europe must guarantee quality, accessible and affordable cancer care for all its citizens, more eu research funding, the exchange of best practice, treatment research and the european health data space and cancer treatment centres of excellence. we also need to make it easier for cancer patients to seek treatment in another eu country. quality cancer care is for every european, regardless of income, gender, place of residence or ethnic background. cancer treatments and drugs, especially those for very rare cancers, are expensive. we should therefore use the european system of joint procurement for the procurement of cancer medicines, which was used for the procurement of corona vaccines. it would make treatment more affordable for both patients and health care systems.
in writing. the report of the special committee of the european parliament, which was drawn up mainly on the initiative of the european people's party, is the result of a year and a half of negotiations. the outcome contains a number of useful recommendations that can help patients in treatment, speed up research or strengthen prevention. however, the report also contained a number of proposals which complicated the achievement of the objectives set and interfered with the freedom of choice of the individual. one of them is a global ban on the promotion of sporting events by beer and spirits producers. such a ban would jeopardise the functioning of small and local sporting events or sports clubs, particularly in times of pandemic. i therefore made an additional proposal to limit the prohibition to events with a predominantly children's audience.
the agenda includes the discussion of statements by the council and the commission on the surveillance of politicians, prosecutors, lawyers and journalists, as well as other natural persons and entities in the eu member states through the use of computer surveillance software (2022/2546 ((rsp)). i would like to inform the honourable members that a round of speeches by the political groups is planned for this debate. i would also remind you that there is no catch-the-eye procedure for all the discussions of this part-session, nor will there be any blue-card questions. in addition, as in the recent sessions, remote assistance is provided by parliament's liaison offices in the member states. i would also like to remind you that the speakers in the chamber will continue to be from the centre stage and i would therefore ask members to check the list of speakers and to approach the centre stage as their speaking time approaches. i now give the floor to the under-secretary for european affairs, mr clément beaune, on behalf of the council.
president of the council. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to thank you for putting this debate on such a sensitive and important subject on the agenda of your session. i simply want to say, first of all, that the use of surveillance software to infiltrate, in particular, phones is obviously unacceptable and must be condemned in the strongest possible terms. i want to make this simple and clear. the european union is founded on core values that bring us together: democracy, respect for the rule of law, freedom of expression and information, freedom of the media and the protection of privacy are among them. these are all, among others, fundamental rights enshrined in our primary law, in our charter. the european institutions all and the member states each have a joint responsibility and duty to ensure that these rights and freedoms are protected against unlawful surveillance on a daily basis. of course, in all our democracies, some frameworks, some limitations on the right to privacy may be necessary. it is the collective life that imposes it for certain purposes, in particular for the fight against crime, in some cases, but always, and this is the meaning and strength of our democracies, always limitations are necessary, they are also strictly framed, in particular by european rules on the protection of human rights. the use of surveillance measures can only be an exceptional measure and the use of surveillance measures in question constitutes such an intrusion into privacy that the highest and most stringent safeguards must be complied with. as you know, in our legal system it is up to each member state to carry out the necessary investigations, if any, in accordance with eu law, to combat any surveillance measures which are illegal. this is essential to create or restore a climate of trust, which we see every day as necessary and sometimes damaged. a journalist, in particular, must always feel safe in europe to contact a source and carry out his investigative work. a political opponent must always be allowed to express his views. a human rights activist must always feel able to defend his or her cause, free from any political, public or private pressure or interference. the situation is also a reminder of our collective vulnerability - and i say this with gravity - in terms of cybersecurity. cybersecurity is not just about protecting economic activities or critical infrastructure, even though it is also vital to our sovereignty. cybersecurity is also about protecting the electronic devices of our daily lives, such as smartphones, which have often become an extension of ourselves and which process so much data that they reveal our private, personal or professional life. the european union is also stepping up its efforts in the security sector. i repeat: this is a priority of the french presidency of the council of the european union. i would like to emphasize in this connection, to be very specific, the intensive work currently being carried out with the european parliament to achieve - we hope, we are actively working on it - an agreement on the directive on the security of information systems, sometimes called nis 2, during the french presidency. this is an objective which we would very much like to achieve with the european parliament and the support of the european commission. there is another trilogue meeting this week. i think that our debate on this is also timely. i thank you for your commitment, for this debate and for your attention. i shall of course be at your disposal to answer all the questions raised in the course of the debate.
member of the commission. madam president, mr minister, ladies and gentlemen, allow me to repeat in the context of this debate what commission president ursula von der leyen said about the pegasus case. i quote: 'the commission strongly condemns any illegal access to the communication system or any form of illegal interception of user communications. other any attempt by national security services to unlawfully access the data of individuals, whether lawyers, journalists, prosecutors, civil society activists or political opponents, is unacceptable. of course, new technologies can play a vital role for the police and the judiciary, but only if they are used in a proportionate way and in accordance with all applicable law. national security cannot be a white-wash for infringing the fundamental rights of europeans. the media must be able to work freely, independently and without fear of reprisals, everywhere in the european union. free political opposition is crucial to democracy. as you know, under european law it is the member states which are responsible for protecting their national security. however, this does not mean that member states are exempted from their obligations under union law on the sole ground that they claim to act in the interests of national security. in other words, the fact that a measure is presented as being related to national security does not mean that everything is permissible. the european court of justice has repeatedly made this point. according to its case law, it is for the member states alone to define their essential security interests and to adopt appropriate measures to protect them. the mere fact that a national measure has been taken for the purpose of protecting national security cannot render eu law inapplicable. the court has made it clear that it is for the member states to substantiate such a claim. member states must supervise and control their security services and must ensure that they fully respect fundamental rights, including privacy and the protection of personal data. there is also a need to create a favourable environment for media freedom, which includes safeguarding the safety of journalists and protecting them from threats, including online. any attempts by national security services to illegally access the data of citizens, including journalists and political opponents is unacceptable. independent supervisory authorities, such as national data protection authorities and national courts, have an important role to play in that respect. the commission expects them to make full use of all their supervisory powers to thoroughly investigate the allegations regarding the pegasus spyware and restore citizens' trust. in this context, i am aware that the hungarian data protection authority announced the conclusion of its investigation into the matter. we understand that an investigation by the budapest regional office of the hungarian prosecution service is still ongoing. i'm also aware that in poland, the senate special committee is examining the use of pegasus. i would like to assure you that the commission is continuing to follow the issue closely and that we are gathering information in this regard. when member states apply eu law, they must comply with the fundamental rights enshrined in the eu charter of fundamental rights and even when union law is not applicable, the privacy of communications and the protection of personal data are still subject to strict safeguards under the european convention on human rights and the constitutional laws of the member states. in particular, any restrictions or exceptions to fundamental rights must be duly justified, necessary and proportionate. we are particularly aware of the specific risks sometimes faced by opposition politicians, journalists and human rights defenders in this regard. with regard specifically to the media, the commission adopted a recommendation on the safety of journalists in september last year. one specific recommendation of this text states that member states should ensure full implementation of the european and national legal frameworks on the confidentiality of communications and online privacy, with a view to ensuring that journalists and other media professionals are not subject to illegal online tracking or surveillance. furthermore, the recommendation provides that member states should, upon request, assist journalists who seek to determine whether their devices or online accounts have been compromised in obtaining the services of cybersecurity forensic investigators. the commission will monitor the implementation by the member states of this and other recommendations. we call on all member states to implement legislation and safeguards to protect individuals from any unlawful surveillance, including any arbitrary or mass surveillance. ladies and gentlemen, the european union has a strong legal framework for data protection and privacy. this framework must be respected. as i have stressed, the authorities of the member states are obliged to supervise and control their security services and law enforcement agencies to ensure that they fully respect fundamental rights, including the protection of personal data, privacy and freedom of expression. in so far as european rules are applicable, the fundamental rights enshrined in the charter must be respected. where union law is not applicable, member states remain bound by the guarantees provided for in the european convention on human rights and national constitutions. i would also like to stress that a swift adoption of the eprivacy regulation will further harmonise privacy rules in the single market and strengthen the enforcement powers of the competent authorities. we therefore invite the co-legislators to work constructively towards the swift adoption of this regulation. i thank you and will of course be very happy to answer your questions, but above all to listen to your observations and comments.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, if anything became clear in these past weeks, it is that we have yet to discover the full depths of this pegasus spyware scandal, so it’s very good and important that we have this debate here today. there are three points that i would like to make. first, that we cannot overemphasise the severity of this scandal. i think it’s important for all of us to put ourselves in the place of the victim, to try and imagine that every message you send to your loved ones is being read by the government, that every video you watch on your phone is being watched with you, that people who do not have your best interests at heart know your exact whereabouts at any given moment. this should already immensely scare you. it does scare me, but my fear is nothing compared with the fear of journalists scrutinising not-so-democratic governments, of opposition politicians, activists, ngos, lawyers, all those who are considered an inconvenience by the powers that be in their countries. that is why it is important that this european parliament will always stand behind and fight alongside the victims of these attacks, and that we do so with strength and vigour. because the effects of such attacks are wider than the direct victims. it has a chilling effect on anybody that wants to speak out, scrutinise governments and defend democracy and rule of law and that once enjoyed the democratic freedom of fighting an election in a free and fair manner. it directly undermines democracy and the rule of law, and it cannot go unchallenged. and this is why the inquiry committee that will be set up in this house is a very timely and important step. second point that i’d like to make is that this is not about national security; it is about rule of law. it is not about whether countries are allowed or not to use technology to fight organised crime and terrorism – because technological innovation can and must play an important role in keeping us all safe. we already see that criminals and terrorists deploy increasingly advanced technology, and law enforcement needs to be able to fight them at least on a level playing field. but that, once again, is not what this is about. this is about the abuse of technology for political gain and about the complete absence of proper checks and balances. thirdly, this is not only a polish or a hungarian issue, it is a european issue and in fact, it’s a global issue. and we had a very interesting hearing from the epp group last week with experts and victims of the pegasus scandal. what struck me most was a comment by a polish lawyer, roman giertych, himself a victim of the polish government’s abuse of pegasus, and he said that this was not about the victims. the victims, in his words, are the canary in the coalmine. they are the first symptom. they are the warning of a much wider problem. they are an alarm that rings loudly for everyone to hear, and the eu must now show that we have heard this alarm. we must act upon the warning we have received and defend democracy and the rule of law whenever and wherever necessary.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, commissioner, a maxim of scientific progress teaches us that, inexorably, everything that can be done that is within the reach of historically available technology will simply be done. and that the only brake against that determination is the one imposed by ethics and by the civilized rule of law. neither barrier is immune to criminal practices and unscrupulous rulers. i think both threats are present in the subject of this debate. a technology made available to no less than forty countries, as we have learned, by an israeli patent security company (nso), under the alias pegasus, that allows an unprecedented intrusion into the fundamental rights that we hold most dear: privacy, the confidentiality of communications, privacy of life. in short, the security of our personal data and our communications through, precisely, the technological tool with the greatest storage capacity ever housed in the history of mankind with regard to these precious assets: mobile phones. a technology that is able to activate, without us knowing it, the microphone or the camera, thus becoming a cyber surveillance device 24 hours a day. this e-privacy technology is grossly incompatible with european law, grossly violating our standard regulated by european law, the general data protection regulation, and of course the directive on data protection in the criminal field, which accompanies it precisely to make it possible for the agencies that have that responsibility to investigate crimes: prosecutors, police, forces and security bodies. because in no case can security be used as a pretext for violating fundamental rights. the history of this civilized rule of law has taught us this, which excludes evidence obtained illegally and prohibits its validation in court. we have therefore a lot to do and we do not have to close the door to any response within the reach of this european parliament, including, of course, that special committee of inquiry to ensure that the commission exercises its role not only as a guarantor of european law, as a guardian of the treaties and of european law, including european privacy law. it includes, commissioner, in its annual report on the rule of law, the failure to comply with european standards, which are the highest in the world in the protection of fundamental rights. this also includes the procedural guarantee of exclusion of any communication unlawfully obtained for any purpose. it's strictly forbidden. the 'doctrine of the poisoned tree' is called in procedural law, which includes in european criminal law, on the legal basis of article 83, a european criminal offence of illicit use of spyware, cyber surveillance, and therefore mass cyber surveillance. there's so much to do, we can't rule out any possibility. but i mean, in the same way that, whatever can be technologically attempted will be done, there will always be unscrupulous rulers. and we regret that the list of usual suspects, in addition to other countries of dubious democratic character, includes two member states of the european union, hungary and poland, again. not only is this completely unacceptable, but the european union has an obligation to do everything in its power to protect and guarantee the fundamental rights of europeans, those protected by the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, which lays down not only the privacy and confidentiality of personal data, but also the rules of legality, necessity, proportionality and lawfulness of the object, legitimacy of the objective, for any measure that may affect the fundamental rights of citizens and of any person to whom european law applies. let us never forget that the charter of fundamental rights of the european union protects not only europeans but all people in the application of european law.

on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, i find that this whole situation reminds me very much of a movie that i think many of us know, das leben der anderen (‘the lives of others’), which describes how a government critic was being spied upon by a government agent around the clock, and we all found it a very chilling image. but the point is that it’s not a movie. this has been a reality for millions of europeans for many decades and i get the feeling that europe today is being haunted by the spectre of its totalitarian past. this is not an incident. it’s not something that we have to treat in in a single debate and then it will go away. it goes against everything that the european union stands for. as a matter of fact, this is about why we want to be in the european union. i find it remarkable that the us government has very swiftly blacklisted nso, stating that nso has ‘developed and supplied software to foreign governments that used these tools to maliciously target government officials, journalists, businesspeople, activists [and] academics ... such practices threaten the rules—based international order’. but, in europe, where this is happening, the council and the commission have so far been silent. yes, you have condemned the practices, but what has actually been done? nothing. european governments spying on their own citizens for political purposes is totally and wholly unacceptable. using national security or the fight against terrorism, or whatever other security—related argument, as a pretext for this practice, colleagues, is textbook autocracy and evident rubbish. it doesn’t only affect the persons targeted, but it affects the whole of society because it has a chilling effect. when no one can be sure anymore that he is not being spied upon, more and more people will start practising self-censorship. we have already gone through that in europe. never again. we will also need to have a very close look at the possible impact that these practices may have had on the integrity of elections in europe, and notably the 2019 european elections in poland. i’ve heard powerful statements today condemning these practices, but the funny thing is that, at the same time, we are negotiating the eprivacy directive, the very piece of legislation that is supposed to protect people and restrict the powers that governments have to spy on their own citizens, and that, frankly, i find that not everybody who spoke is always on our side. the european parliament position is very clear: we want those restrictions to remain in place. so i hope that i will find the council – and the epp group and the ecr group and others who have voted against the european parliament position – on our side in the eprivacy trilogues because, for too long, i think that critical questions have been dismissed as left—wing whining, and i think these cases demonstrate very clearly why this is a very real issue. finally, i am very pleased that the european parliament has decided to set up an inquiry committee. my group, the renew group, already proposed this months ago, but only after the revelations about poland did a majority emerge. but i can assure everybody that, even if the european parliament doesn’t have the same powers as national parliaments when it comes to parliamentary inquiries, we will play a lead role in this. we will stand by the european citizens. we will defend democracy. we will defend freedom and we will defend justice, and i count on all my colleagues for doing so.
for the greens/efa group madam president, ladies and gentlemen, alongside an increasingly invasive surveillance capitalism, the surveillance state is now sneaking in, shamelessly attacking our fundamental freedoms. it is no longer just the mass surveillance programmes of china or the united states that are to be feared, but also the targeted cyber espionage that is being carried out by an increasing number of governments, inside and outside the european union. this pegasus scandal reveals nothing less than the illegal and abusive use of these cyber surveillance weapons on our fellow citizens. make no mistake about it: pegasus is not a spy affair among the powers of this world. this scandal illustrates the risk to each and every one of us of being watched, including you and me. the official mission of these spyware programs is to monitor individuals suspected of terrorism or organized crime. however, the pegasus scandal shows that such targeting is used outside any legal framework, including within the european union. women and politicians, journalists, prosecutors, lawyers, human rights activists... we are all affected. cyber espionage technologies provide those who use them with a virtual pass-through to all our personal data. imagine that every time you take your smartphone out of your pocket, you're not alone, reading your messages, listening to your conversations, viewing your photos and videos, being able to activate your camera, your microphone or your geolocation. and then, when you realize that your life is being spied on without your knowledge, these questions strike you: why am i being spied on? how did they get access to my phone? what did they see? what do they know? who are they, anyway? this was the experience of a belgian human rights activist who was probably under surveillance by moroccan services. this feeling of insecurity was reported by a belgian journalist and his rwandan wife, who were probably hacked by the kigali authorities. these are the questions that are tormenting those who have been spied on by their governments in hungary and poland because they inform, campaign, defend, oppose. the u.s. has put the parent company of pegasus on a blacklist. the un is calling for a moratorium on the sale and transfer of this technology. it is time, more than time, for the european union to act. i can only agree with what sophie in 't veld has just said, and first of all that we understand what is at stake. we therefore call for the establishment of a european parliament committee of inquiry to shed light on the extent of these illegal cyber-hacking practices, starting with the identification of which member states possess pegasus or equivalent spyware. responsibilities must be established and sanctions imposed accordingly. this committee of inquiry is an essential step towards a regulatory framework for the conditions of export, sale, transfer and, above all, use of cyber-surveillance technologies. the question is: how can we still conclude agreements on police or judicial cooperation with states that spy on us and on our fellow citizens? ladies and gentlemen - and i conclude, madam president - we are all aware of the risks inherent in these technologies, which are now being used to monitor, intimidate, silence and, ultimately, endanger our democracies. we must protect our fellow citizens from this new era of surveillance, and that starts with getting the truth about these egregious violations of our fundamental rights. i can only join sophie in calling on my colleagues, whether from the epp, the social democrats, the ecr or other groups, to join us in setting up this committee of inquiry. i think that is where the european parliament can play its most useful role, before, of course, legislating with you, commissioner.
but i would ask you, mr president, to remain on the subject of the debate, to say nothing else, and to be respectful of the members who have spoken and are listening to you. if you do not intervene on the substance, i shall be obliged to take your word. i repeat that we must stick to the substance of the matter under discussion and that is not respectful of this house or of the members who are speaking, so i hope that this will not happen again.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the the commission is not prepared to accept this. we should be clear about what we're talking about with pegasus, a spyware that lights people up, turns them into glass. and once it's infected, it grants complete access to all the data on the phone, can turn cameras, microphones on and off without us even knowing. this is the perfect surveillance. it's used to categorize people, to put them in danger levels without them knowing it. this is total surveillance, as george orwell described it with horror. there is nothing, there is nothing in the world, not even counterterrorism, that justifies total surveillance - especially not in a democracy. because what we see is what happens: journalists, bloggers, oppositionists, lawyers, dissidents are spied on. that's what this is about. it's not just our right to privacy that's at stake here. it is about the essence of democracy and our freedoms as a whole. worse, governments like poland, hungary -- yes, germany -- are using this software, and about 50 countries around the world. and there is no shortage of perversity when it comes to using money to buy pegasus for victims of crime. we have to ask ourselves: what kind of society do we want to live in when mass surveillance becomes the norm? such a society has left the soil of the rule of law. i don't think we all want that. after nsa, tempora, after scandals of illustrious intelligence agencies, the alarm bells must finally ring. we are demanding full clarification, a full parliamentary committee of inquiry with all the resources, binding consequences for the member states and not just rhetoric. we need clear rules. we need to ban the manufacture, sale and export of mass surveillance technology. we need to talk about this, not whine.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the recent revelations about the pegasus project have highlighted the seriousness of the global threat of human rights violations through secret computer surveillance. the pegasus spyware, which was sold by a company that fights crime and terrorism, is just one of many tools accused of illegally targeting and spying on politicians, activists, judges, journalists or other groups of individuals. anyone could be abused, with serious repercussions on fundamental freedoms and even personal security. there is a veritable surveillance industry, a marketplace of widespread secrecy that offers low-cost and easily accessible services and technologies. it is therefore extremely important that there should be full clarity on this type of activity, so that the lack of transparency and accountability of companies and users is addressed. there is a need for strict regulation and effective controls on destination states, contracts and any kind of information which may reveal the misuse of these products and the possible violation of fundamental rights.
member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, thank you for this debate. i know that it has been the driving force in parliament for some time now, and we are also paying particular attention to it in the commission. first of all, i would like to say that there is indeed a debate on the very strong legal framework we already have in the union for the protection of privacy and personal data. so this is a debate that can continue. moreover, you have also been involved in it several times as co-legislators, because the opposition between security and freedom is an ongoing debate. i would also like to point out that technology is of course sometimes used for good by security services, provided that it is used in a legal, recognised and abusive manner - as i mentioned earlier. i therefore strongly emphasise that there is a real need to move forward in the continuation of the debate on legislative frameworks, such as the 'privacy and electronic communications' framework, which will obviously not answer all the questions you have raised, but which would provide a better framework for the approaches you are highlighting today. beyond this discussion of the legal framework for privacy protection - mr lenaers alluded to the strong and robust side of this framework - there is a real problem of implementation. i would remind you that, even within the framework of european legislation, this responsibility for implementation lies with the member states. i would like to make it clear again that we are of course following the action of the member states closely. i reminded him, there was an investigation - if i am correctly informed, on his own initiative - by the data protection authority in hungary; there is a prosecutorial investigation in hungary; there's also a move from the polish senate. we will therefore continue to monitor and seek information on the progress of these various investigations. we will also support any other approach, including the one that mrs veld and mr lamberts have called for in relation to parliament's investigations. of course, we will also be very attentive to any information that may be gathered in connection with these various investigations, whether national or carried out in your parliament. the third point i want to stress is the cooperation we need, and cooperation, first of all - i repeat - with the national authorities. of course, if there are indications of a lack of independence or inefficiency, either from data protection authorities or from judicial authorities, we will not hesitate, as we have already done, to first make our comments in the context of the rule of law report mr lópez aguilar referred to this. if there are indeed relevant elements, they will be included in the rule of law report. i would also remind you that this year, for the first time, the report will also include recommendations. if necessary, we will introduce infringement procedures. i would like to remind you that we do not hesitate to initiate infringement proceedings: recently, the announcement of an infringement procedure concerning a member state has helped to put in order the problems of incompatibility within a data protection authority. if you have concrete information on other situations across the union, we would be happy to receive it, because we have not received complaints about a number of the authorities that have been mentioned today. so cooperation with the member states sometimes also involves you: when you have specific information, do not hesitate to send it to us. finally, on the debate on the development and then the import or use of a number of new technologies or software, the commission is of course open to thinking about how to use the existing legislative framework and perhaps develop it in the future, but always distinguishing between support for innovation and technological development and how these technologies are used. because, i repeat, when we had debates on the fight against terrorism, for example, we all wanted the authorities and the security services to have effective tools for collecting information. on the other hand, whenever there is abuse, i believe that we should be able to combat it, which does not preclude decisions on the import or prohibition of certain technologies. but then we will have to discuss with you and with the council whether new regulations are to be introduced in this area. i am sure that this debate will not end today.
president of the council. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to return to some of the points raised in the debate. first, since some examples or cases of national problems have been mentioned, i would like to point out that, whatever one thinks, it is not for the council to take a position at this stage on the work of a national authority which is in progress. but, as commissioner reynders pointed out, a number of procedures have been opened by the national authorities - this is the case in hungary. i would also point out that the proper functioning of data protection authorities is not only an option but also an obligation for the member states in the implementation of the general data protection regulation, and that the control of this implementation by the national authorities is carried out under the ultimate control of the european commission. when there are failures or malfunctions, infringement procedures or various legal procedures can of course be initiated. i want to return to a number of instruments, since we still have a lot of room for manoeuvre and responsibility which, as i said just now, is a matter for national prerogatives. this does not mean that there is no community control and that the various institutions do not have responsibilities to exercise, as i have mentioned for the commission. commissioner reynders raised the issue of imports of a number of sensitive dual-use goods or software which may amount to espionage. there is no specific european framework in this area today - this is also a national responsibility - but the european commission can point to a number of difficulties. we have a toolbox here that can be mobilized. when we look at how to strengthen these possibilities for intervention, i believe that there are also a number of elements that can be mentioned or mobilized. firstly - and this is obviously not the responsibility of the council or the presidency of the council, as mr lamberts and mrs veld have mentioned - these are internal discussions and decisions which you have to take - a committee of inquiry, for example a parliamentary one, can be set up. there are also legislative texts which can - in some cases, which have been debated - strengthen our arsenal. this was mentioned by sophie in t veld among others. i think about the texts on "e-privacy" - i say it in bad english - or on electronic evidence, "e-evidence" - i say it always in rather bad english - where we hope to move the work forward and we are committed to it, beyond hope. on the subject of e-privacy, the technical negotiations have finally resumed, and i believe we have some sensitivities, on data retention in particular, but we are confident about the possibility of engaging the trilogues during the french presidency's six-month term. similarly, my colleague, the keeper of the seals and minister of justice, éric dupond-moretti, has made the issue of electronic evidence, e-evidence, a very important priority in the 'justice and home affairs' sector. we hope that we will be able to launch the trilogues during the semester and take these discussions forward. in my opening remarks i referred to the work on the nis 2 directive, which is also very important in the debates we are having this morning, and we have, as i said, trilogues which are continuing this week. there are also a number of judicial, jurisdictional or more political tools, in the sense of the debate, on the rule of law in particular. on this last point, as commissioner reynders rightly pointed out, we now have recommendations in the next edition of the european commission's report, but also in the regular dialogues which are organised by waves of countries within the general affairs council. five countries will be examined again in march, i am sure, including several which were mentioned in this morning's business, and this examination will therefore continue in the general affairs council, with information from the european commission, and which also allows monitoring, vigilance and even pressure to be exerted on these issues of protection of the most fundamental rights and personal data. i was referring above all to the fact that we are a union of laws, both singular and plural, that we still have legal proceedings in our countries which can be brought, and that the national court is also the court of the law of the union. this is sometimes forgotten. i am taking france as an example, but i know that other countries have been involved in the same procedure: several french prosecutors have, on the basis of information they considered sufficient, linked to pegasus in particular, initiated information or legal proceedings which may, if necessary, lead to sanctions, in accordance with national and european rules, obviously in compliance with judicial procedures and the independence of the judiciary. so i think we already have a considerable toolbox today, and i hope that we can do useful work with the french presidency of the council this semester to advance our work and strengthen these tools, at least on the three texts i mentioned.
