president of colombia. good morning to you all, and my special greeting to the president of the european parliament, mrs roberta metsola. i also greet all the members of the bureau of the european parliament, all the members of the conference of presidents, mr borrell and all the members of our delegation. i would like to begin by thanking you for this important invitation to colombia to share in this invaluable organ of multilateralism. i also want to honour the memory of mr sassoli, who had invited us to this place months before. i want to emphasize that this is a breast of democracy, but, above all, it is a platform for the world, to hear our ideas and to build together. ladies and gentlemen of the house, almost forty years ago, in the beautiful city of stockholm, one of the most important spanish-speaking writers came to receive the nobel prize for literature. gabriel garcía márquez, with that beautiful inspiration of magical realism, was preparing to speak at that moment about the loneliness of latin america. four decades have passed, and latin america has gradually overcome that loneliness. it has been integrating into the world and has acquired an increasingly noticeable behavior in the international concert. in fact, our presence before the european parliament is the demonstration of how a latin american nation strengthens its ties, its relations and is able to build a better future, understanding that the international relationship is a vibrant and dynamic state policy that builds, from the evolution of public policies, future, employment, growth and opportunities. there is no doubt that latin america today can show all the developing countries that in these four decades it has made unquestionable progress. latin america has shown that it has increased life expectancy, that it has increased its middle class, that it has also been able to increase, as never before, coverage in health and education, that it has managed to transition to market economies where there is private investment in infrastructure, in energy, and where we are seeing the concept of multilatinism grow, in our own region and in the world. but we cannot fail to express that our region has gone through the most difficult and painful times, as can be seen with the devastating effects of the covid-19 pandemic. this invisible enemy has invaded our lives and put our health care system in jeopardy, as it has done for many of the nations represented here. this invisible enemy also affected our economic dynamism, it led us to close down many of the activities that are part of our daily lives and it also affected us in multiple social indicators. that enemy broke in and forced us to have to react with speed. it also put at risk many of the public policies we had developed for the welfare of the people. and many tried to put on the table a false dilemma: the dilemma of life and health or the economy and employment. that dilemma does not exist because without good health there is no development and without development there is no good health. and we had to take on those balances in a dynamic and determined way. but latin america has also shown the resilience to face those storms and has also shown that it can bring effective responses to each of its citizens. i want to put that challenge on behalf of colombia and tell you how a country like ours has had to face these circumstances without letting any of the dreams, any of the policies that we had proposed, be taken away from us. we closed 2019 with the highest economic growth in five years, we managed to have a primary fiscal surplus for the first time in almost eight years and we had achieved, among others, the best historical year of tourism, with more than 4.5 million non-resident visitors. we came to 2020, and we saw growth in january and february, when our economy managed to exceed 4%. but then the pandemic came along and it took our economy down 6.8 percent, and we lost a lot of loved ones. and there, our nation's resilience was tested. we could have calmed down in the face of those storms or bowed in the face of uncertainty, but we didn't. i am speaking to you today to tell you that, in terms of public health, a country like colombia has faced this pandemic by doubling the number of intensive care units, from 5,400 to more than 13,000. it has managed to go from one laboratory with the capacity to do test processing under the berlin protocol to having more than 186 laboratories throughout the territory. he has succeeded in developing a telemedicine practice, with which we have gone from thousands to more than ten million citizens who today, month after month, receive their medical care virtually or at home. and we also managed to build a national vaccination plan under the principle of equity, and massive and free, which today gives us 80% of the population with at least one dose. and we're close to closing this month of february with over 70% with double doses and over seven million booster shots that have been deployed throughout the national territory. we want to close this semester with more than 90% and get that message to all the colombian people: that in the most difficult moment it was equity that guided this vaccination policy. but at the same time, honourable members, we had to face up to the effects on the economy. and colombia, who is speaking to you today, yesterday received a news item: we will achieve in 2021 the greatest economic growth in our history. and it's not a rebound, but a dynamic revival, because we were one of the fastest growing economies in the world, but we did it thanks to having launched what we call the colombian new deal, deploying public, private and public-private investment throughout the territory, and also making economic transfers, going from three million beneficiaries to ten million beneficiary households and having subsidized 40 or 50 percent of a legal minimum monthly wage to more than four million workers. today, that colombia which is seeing economic dynamism has european investment. the investment is seen in infrastructure projects, where we can also say that by august this year our country will have inaugurated infrastructure projects where the second lanes, second roads and third lanes will equal in just four years what the first twenty-five years of concessions were. thanks also to the investment of this continent and hand in hand with national investors in the midst of the pandemic, we are moving towards the energy transition, from 0.2% of our energy matrix in non-conventional renewables to close to 20% which, added to the water source, puts us with one of the cleanest energy matrices on the planet. and we are also proud that we have achieved the largest housing sales in the history of colombia, but marked by the sales of housing of social interest, 61% of whose beneficiaries are families who have incomes below two minimum wages. that vibrant and vigorous colombia that responds to this challenge also managed, in the midst of the pandemic, to achieve the largest agricultural exports in its history, with europe being one of the main markets. those open markets, the product of a free trade agreement, meant closing gaps and meant giving that unparalleled opportunity to those farmers who joined agriculture on contract to sell without intermediaries and reach the big markets of the world. but the colombia i am talking about is also a colombia that in the midst of the pandemic developed a social transformation and that today also makes us proud. in the midst of the pandemic, we managed to launch the free public higher education program, which is now state policy, and which allows us to bring that free higher education enrollment to 97 percent of those enrolled, which allows us to shelter not only the most vulnerable sectors, but the emerging middle class. and, in parallel with that policy, we have achieved that in our country, with this government, the greatest attention is given in terms of income to workers, achieving the largest real increase in the minimum wage consolidated by any government in more than four decades, maintaining its purchasing power and also showing our citizens that, if our economy grows vigorously, we also have to recognize the effort of workers. this determined action, which is also accompanied by this basic emergency income, which we call "solidarity income", and which has represented a revolution in terms of reaching deep colombia with economic transfers and which is added to the return of value added tax (vat) to the most vulnerable families, also allows us to say that in our government 54% of all economic transfers that have been turned in colombia in twenty years of history of this type of transfers have been allocated. and, of course, we must also incorporate peace with legality into that reality. because peace is not left unattended for a single moment in the midst of a pandemic. we've secured over three billion dollars of investment in the regions that have been historically hit hardest by violence. we have put into operation 14 of the 16 territorial development plans and have achieved the largest handover of rural property titles in the history of our country, with 50,000 titles. we have incorporated land into our land banks, to seek equity in distribution and also link them with jobs related to rural productivity. but we have also made bets so that in the construction of peace with legality indigenous communities can contract directly with the state for projects that are relevant in their environment. we extended the victims act for another ten years and we have made collective and individual reparations that already mark historical milestones as well. and that colombia that embraces peace with legality also celebrated last year five years and the implementation was recognized by the united nations and also by the european union, with the brilliant and exhaustive participation of eamon gilmore, the head of its delegation, who said, without offense, that colombia is a reference and is a reference because this implementation that must be advanced in four periods shows that it is able to arrive not with political colors, but with the tricolor of our country, the one that unites us, the yellow, blue and red that represents the presence of the state in every corner. honourable members, these measures are also taking place in the midst of the worst migration crisis that latin america has ever experienced. and although we are not a rich country, we have been able to take care of the migrant. without being swayed by xenophobia or indifference, we have welcomed 1.8 million migrants and are handing out to our venezuelan brothers and sisters these cards that accredit them with temporary protection status. (applause) the venezuelan brothers and sisters who have arrived in colombia are receiving this attention in a campaign we call "visible", so that they can be people, that they can have a professional life, that they can enter the health system, that they can acquire a home, that they can feel sheltered and protected. and, without a doubt, we have received support from the european union at the donor tables and we have had a lot of coordination and participation from the bilateral cooperation agencies. but i must also make an appeal, because it is necessary, and not only to colombia, but to all the countries that have received the migrant population. the speed of commitments and disbursements shows a great gulf. we need to speed up the disbursements from the donor tables to accompany the efforts of all nations. these are not resources we ask for ourselves, but resources that are asked for migrants. and the speed of disbursement also means the continuing confidence in the dynamic cooperation that is required. i cannot help mentioning that as we speak of this humanitarian gesture, it is also necessary that the world continues to act so that the cause of this tragedy is stopped. and the cause of this tragedy is the opprobrious dictatorship of nicolás maduro, which is generating more diaspora, more scourge and more deterioration of the social fabric in venezuela. (applause) honourable members of parliament, the colombia that speaks to you today is also the colombia that proudly feels the second most biodiverse nation on the planet. and together we must tackle the climate crisis that weighs us down. this is no longer an optional path. if we don't act, the effects of the climate crisis will be irreversible. that's why our nation, which only accounts for 0.6 percent of global greenhouse gas emissions, but is one of the most vulnerable nations to the effects of the climate crisis, was there in glasgow to say that we are going to be carbon neutral by 2050, and to reduce 51 percent of our emissions by 2030, and that this year, not 2030, this year we will declare 30 percent of our territory as protected area to show that commitment and to validate that quick and timely action is an example for everyone and that if a country that only accounts for that 0.6 percent of emissions does that, so should the nations that have contributed the most to this crisis. we've joined those efforts with the planting of 180 million trees, with the energy transition, with clean mobility, and with a political act worthy of admiration amidst so many debates that are characteristic of politics today. we got unanimous approval of a climate action law that positions colombia. and it was with that same attitude that last week in brest, alongside president macron and other leaders, we were raising the urgent need to extend the marine protected area and to tackle practices like trawling that is destroying coral reefs that are home to 45 percent of marine life. today, dear members of parliament, we are here to tell you that the changes colombia has undergone have also been the result of its entry into the multilateral environment. europe has supported us with free trade agreements, with cooperation. it accompanies us in the implementation of peace with legality and it accompanies us also in the climate action agenda. colombia is now the 37th country in the oecd. and it's the only partner that nato has in latin america. but colombia is also the interim president of the andean community of nations, the pacific alliance and also of prosur. we believe in multilateralism and we are confident that multilateralism will always be the best answer and will always be the best of alliances when we have experienced it with you. i feel it is my duty to also make a reference to one of the greatest challenges facing latin america. our democracy, regional democracy, cannot be taken for granted because we have to be aware of the threats that exist. today we know that there are three enemies of democracies in the world: populism, post-truth and polarization, as moses naim described in his latest book. those three enemies are present. and those three enemies are the ones who want, many times, to make us part of a geopolitical debate of those who want to fracture the world order. we have to be able to protect our democracy from foreign influence or interference, from those who want to manipulate algorithms, or from those who want to create hatred and division. to stand united in warning of these circumstances is our ethical duty and we have to be able to reject any outside attempt that wants to take our electoral systems to become an experiment of hackers trained to mobilize and detonate hatred. the european union is now accompanying colombia in its electoral process with an observer mission. and we will be working with you to ensure the soundness of our democracy. the european parliament has a duty to support the efforts of the latin american countries to achieve a peaceful settlement of the conflict. the solution to the climate crisis requires latin america as that place that houses the amazon, and that has more than 70 percent of the world's wastelands, and that has underwater riches and that is also a pantry. that is why it is necessary to re-establish the dialogue at the highest level, between the heads of state of europe and latin america, which has not been held for years. this scenario is not only a necessity, but it also allows us to consolidate the values of defending democracy that have kept the dialogue between latin america and europe vibrant and optimistic. today we have to defend democracy. today we must defend the market economy. today we must defend a sustainable and responsible social policy. and today we must also raise our voices. i address you in times of tension in the world. but let this also be an occasion for latin america to make the following message clear: all nations must be free and sovereign to choose whether or not to join a multilateral organization. but a third country cannot, by force, intimidate or intimidate a nation from exercising its sovereignty. (applause) the world must defend that principle, that freedom to choose sovereignly which bodies and under what prerogatives it binds. and the world must also be united in rejecting any violent intimidation or any exercise of violence. colombia, as i said yesterday at nato, will, in the event of an aggression scenario, support the sanctions that are being discussed at the international conference. not only do we not believe in that solution, but we also do not believe in those who want to relive the chapters of the cold war. ladies and gentlemen, security is a democratic value and a public good. security has no party or political colour. security must be experienced to make the protection and full exercise of freedoms viable. today we are aware that we have to work for this international security, for this regional security and for local security. i am glad to see how colombia has become a better country thanks also to the support of the european union. and i would like to invite you to make this case, which you can see as a success story in which, on the basis of the democratic transitions in our country, these advances are being made, serve to further extend cooperation with latin america. this is an imperative and colombia will always be instrumental in achieving that expansion. almost forty years ago, our nobel prize winner gabriel garcía márquez told the world that latin america was alone. today, latin america has largely overcome that loneliness. but today latin america needs europe more than ever. today i invite you to continue building this multilateral dialogue based on democracy, based on the freedom of choice of citizens and based on the right to build consensus in the midst of difference. because it will always be more important to achieve democracy than to impose autocracy. i am proud as president of colombia to address you and to thank you for what we have been able to build and to show you that our nation, despite the difficulties and adversities, is a nation that continues to look with optimism to the future. the next item is the debate on the statement by the vice-president of the commission and high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy on eu-africa relations (2022/2533 ((rsp)). i would remind members that no spontaneous speeches or blue cards will be accepted in any debate at this sitting. as in the past, parliament will also be able to receive contacts from its liaison offices in the member states. i would also point out that the plenary will continue to be addressed from the central podium, and i would therefore ask you to keep an eye on the list of speakers and to go to the podium shortly before your speaking time.
vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, honourable members of the european parliament, we stand on the eve of the sixth eu-africa summit that will take place in brussels on 17 and 18 february, and it’s a unique occasion that doesn’t happen every day, but it has been delayed for years. a unique occasion to replace europe in the centre of the attention of the african partners – to place or to replace, depends on how you see it. this summit will be held in a context which is certainly unprecedented. challenges of the covid-19 pandemic, increased geopolitical landscape, much more competitive, but also presenting opportunities. for example, what can we do with our eu recent global gateway strategy? we have been seeing how much our two continents are interlinked. i have been saying in kenya the other day that the african problems are our problems. but when we try to help them to solve their problems, it’s not because we are generous, but because their problems are our problems. let’s see what’s happening in the sahel or what’s happening in the horn of africa or what’s happening everywhere in the continent with respect to the demographic evolution. and we have to look at africa with a positive eye, not only through the lens of migration problems. africa is a land of countless opportunities, which are opportunities for us also. they have a young and dynamic human potential and they have unlimited possibilities on renewable energy. if we empower african youth and women, we will be able to promote a more green and digital and equal –sometimes we just say green and digital but i say, green, digital and inclusive, it means equal – growth on both our continents. at the african union summit held on 5 and 6 february this year, the new chair, president macky sall of senegal, showed openness to external partners without exclusivity, provided that they are mutually beneficial and respectful of african development priorities and societal choices. at the same occasion, the chairperson of the african union commission, moussa faki, expressed the hope that summits with partners will focus on concrete and transformative projects. concrete – that’s what we need to be. and that is the dynamic in which we are inserting this african union-european union summit. our objectives in the summit are based on a clear understanding of our respective and mutual interests. we want to strengthen our cooperation by defining joint key priorities and partnerships for the next decade. we will amplify the scope and focus of the team europe approach with the impact of our actions. we have to ensure more visibility to our partners and with a strong united response, a message underpinned by concrete actions, we will demonstrate that the european union is africa’s most trusted partner, and we will thereby also counter negative narrative and disinformation about what the europeans are and what europeans are doing and with the african people. so let me quickly go through some specific actions which we are working on. we are preparing an innovative and forward-looking joint declaration outlining tangible initiatives to be co-owned and implemented jointly in support of a common strategic vision for 2030. in particular, we are working with a team europe spirit – you know what i mean, european union institutions and the member states all together – on six particular deliverables, six strands of work to be translated into concrete projects and flagships. i will go through the six of them just to mention in order to frame our debate if you wish. first, an ambitious global gateway africa-europe investment package taking into account global challenges such as climate change and the current health crisis. second, preparing the health system. third, education systems – big priority. four, tools and solutions for the africa-europe security and stability architecture. more and more, our relationship with africa has to incorporate the security issues because when you see how jihadism is going from the sahel to the gulf of guinea, how instability is widespread from somalia along the indian ocean coast toward mozambique and the south. more and more, we are going to have security concerns to share with them and to help them to face them. fifth, an enhanced and reciprocal partnership on migration and mobility. and sixth, a european union-african union initiative for multilateral action and peace, prosperity and the planet. these are the strands for our work, and we have to build not only words, we have to explain concrete proposals and actions to reinforce our relations. yes, i know that so many things have been said or written about this relation. so many critics, sometimes rightfully, sometimes not. the too low distribution of vaccines or the travel ban that we imposed when the omicron variant raised in south africa are good examples of the first, many critics, rightfully, on the latter, we may show the new instrument that we are shaping to better answer security, in each of our partnerships are critics which are not adequate. summarising, i could say that we, the european union and the african union, do not agree on everything, certainly not. i think that we agree on the essential and this is enough, it is a good, sound basis for renewing a stronger partnership between us. let’s see what’s happening in the summit. we went to kigali to prepare the summit. at the ministerial level meeting, there were some important disagreements. there were some important concerns. that’s the moment to speak to each other, frankly, honestly, in order to improve our partnership, because certainly we need to solve the problems that african people are suffering. and keep in mind, this can be our motto: african problems are our problems, and when we work to try these problems, we work for ourselves also.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, africa is our neighbour, africa is our partner, and both our continents face exceptional challenges posed by the health crisis, economic recession and climate change, as well as population growth and demographic trends. the summit will be decisive if it goes beyond ringing declarations of intent and secures concrete commitments based on mutual strategic priorities – a win—win partnership. to be successful, the ppe group sees three main points as key. firstly, europe and africa have to work together to facilitate significant investment opportunities for business on the african continent that can support the creation of millions of jobs. this is necessary to keep up with africa’s rapidly growing young population. we also need to boost trade. moreover, with our experience in developing the single market, the eu must strongly support africa in the same endeavour. secondly, even more importantly, we need to have access to vaccines and to healthcare in africa. this must include setting up capacities for local vaccine manufacturing and more funding to healthcare. thirdly, we must tackle the root causes of migration jointly and build up cooperation on migration management. to sum up, it is time for a fresh start.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the council, commission and parliament agree on five key areas that need to be addressed in the eu's relationship with africa in order to stop illegal migration. address the root causes, prevent irregular migration, combat trafficking in human beings, facilitate return and reintegration and respect international law. we all know that the current measures are inadequate. hostile neighbors who have used migrants in hybrid warfare draw the same conclusions. the eu needs a new approach. so why is it that when denmark and its social democratic government launch a new proposal, the danish asylum plan, which addresses the five key areas, including respect for the right to asylum, the eu institutions try to put the wheels in motion? 22,000 people have died in the mediterranean since 2014, and africa's population will increase from 1.3 billion to 4 billion by the end of this century. unless you take the example of denmark, present a popularly supported migration policy and start negotiating with african countries on this, illegal migration will continue and more will die.

mr president, i would like to thank you. mr borrell, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. since the wave of freedom that began with the fall of the berlin wall in 1989, we have taken democracy for granted, both inside and outside the eu. but democracy is under threat all over the world. today, more than ever, it is important that we mobilise for democracy, with democracy as the guiding light of our foreign, aid and security policy. democracies have to work together. we have to stand up for each other. a majority of eu member states must be able to take swift decisions to impose sanctions on countries that violate human rights and not be stopped by the occasional interference. the fact that there are political prisoners in europe today, in 2022, is a tragedy. today, 15 february, 1,062 people, including minors, are in prison in belarus for their opinions. the eu will never betray democracy and we will never turn our backs on the prisoners.
mr president, i would like to thank you. my colleagues! we can't let history repeat itself. democracy and human rights must never again become the privilege of a few. the report on human rights and democracy in the world, however, speaks its own language. something unusual is happening right now. we're seeing a decline. the decline is as present in our union as it is globally, and the antidote is leadership and political will. leadership by reversing the negative developments in our own union by strangling the financial support for rogue governments. otherwise, we won't have global credibility. political will by increasing the global toolbox. the new eu global sanctions mechanism is a good thing, but there is still a lot of work to be done. all our member countries must meet the un global target of at least 0.7 percent of gnp for aid. the work to achieve the 17 sustainable development goals of the 2030 agenda must be intensified and qualified majority voting in the european council on human rights violations must be applied.

mr president, i would like to thank you. a toxic-free everyday life for all, and especially our children, should be taken for granted. we should not even have to discuss these matters in parliament. but we need it. because children are surrounded by strange, dangerous substances in their daily lives. there are toxins in the food, the water, the clothes and, unfortunately, not least, in the toys. children face this every day, and it's very difficult for us adults to protect children from it. and they're extra sensitive as they grow, and of course they're exposed extra when they're chewing and examining things, maybe putting them in their mouths. after all, these are toys we're talking about, so of course we adults have a very big responsibility to make sure that the toys are non-toxic. it should not be up to individual parents or grandparents to examine and check whether or not the toys contain toxins. of course, the authorities and the manufacturers must guarantee this. that is why we are voting against all exemptions for dangerous chemicals in toys. the limit values for the chemical content of a toy should apply to all toys, not just toys for very young children or toys designed to be placed in the mouth. if you think about it, it's pretty simple. of course, only toys that are completely safe should be manufactured. the child's perspective and the precautionary principle must apply.
madam president, i would like to thank you. should the eu send anti-tank guns or blankets and field hospitals to ukraine? the guns or the billions of dollars that could disappear? the european commission shall not lend to the budget and shall not spend more than they have. we sweden democrats have voted against budget support for badly managed economies in the eu and outside the eu. the eu has provided budget support to ukraine five times in the past. another $12 billion to kiev is not about the current threat from russia. the commission warns that the money could be squandered because it is not earmarked budget support and can be used freely. the court's indication of widespread corruption in the country should ring alarm bells. instead, we should provide military equipment through the peace facility and punish russia with harsh sanctions if they invade. my own country, sweden, should provide defence equipment, but the social democrats are against it. the reckless handling of taxpayers' money and the false solidarity with the ukrainian people is what it is. when the british send weapons to ukraine, the swedish ss are inspired by the german government who wants to send helmets. it's called hypocrisy.

the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, what is it? the pandemic has been a particularly difficult time for children and young people. many dreams have been put on hold. many have lost their jobs, others have had their opportunities for study or travel cancelled, and children and young people from already vulnerable backgrounds with weaker social protection networks are particularly affected. our children and young people are our future, and our future is determined to a large extent by what we do here and now. now more than ever, we need to listen to our young people, but we also need to take action. the establishment of the european year of youth is therefore very, very positive. it puts pressure on us to work more purposefully for a europe for young people, and as part of being able to shape their own lives and influence the development of society, we must also give priority to living conditions. we in the socialist group want to create more jobs with good conditions and decent wages. we want to ensure high quality education with fair remuneration for internships and apprenticeships. we want to put an end to unpaid internships. yes, there are things we can do. there are things we should do. and then there are things we have to do to be able to see ourselves in the mirror. i want young people in our eu to be able to dream and, above all, to make their dreams come true.

rapporteur. – madam president, dear colleagues, dear commissioner mcguinness, dear minister beaune, integrated capital markets are a prerequisite for sustaining growth over the longer term. i therefore welcome all measures to provide additional input impetus to the european capital markets union. at the same time, digitalisation is rushing through the financial services industry, and to cope with this trend, it is not only vital to make existing financial legislation fit for digital, but also to show an openness towards new technologies that could make financial markets safer and more efficient. the digital finance package is an important milestone in that regard. the technology that underpins crypto assets – the so-called distributed ledgers, or dlt – has a great potential for creative destruction and this will lead to productivity improvements. its possible benefits in the provision of financial services include less complexity, strengthened network resilience, and reduced operational and financial risks. currently, financial markets infrastructures are not authorised to use dlt to issue, trade, and settle financial instruments such as bonds, shares and exchange-traded funds. the dlt pilot regime aims at testing exactly that while also addressing associated risks. the project fully fits the cmu objectives, helps us to keep on track in an evolving digital world and puts the eu at the forefront of innovation. during the trilogues parliament held high six objectives or principles. first, innovation. we followed a ‘sandbox’ approach, meaning that we allow for temporary and conditional exemptions from current legislation. the experience gained that way should foster innovation further. secondly, market integrity. the existing market for financial instruments directive and the central securities depositories regulation apply in a traditional, account-based environment. to ensure adequate regulatory and supervisory requirements for this regime, we checked in detail which rules from both pieces of legislation would also be essential for application in a dlt environment. third principle: investor protection, which i think speaks for itself. fourth element: financial stability. our feeling was that the initial commission proposal was a bit too lenient on financial stability safeguards, as provided for in the csdr. we have significantly strengthened these safeguards. fifth: level playing field. providing equal opportunity to all market players is a fundamental principle of eu legislation. and the sixth and last of the objectives was of course technological neutrality. as a final remark, i would like to address our successors as co-legislators with a few words of caution for the longer term. what works in a limited testing environment does not necessarily work in a broader context. if the project turns out to be successful, simply expanding its scope and changing the permanent regulatory framework for financial services accordingly might create risks that are not noticeable or that were not noticeable in the pilot phase. secondly, future co-legislators should thoroughly scrutinise the exemptions granted under the pilot project before upscaling them. this is notably the case for possible risks linked to settlement in commercial bank money. thirdly, the parliament negotiating team would also have liked a stronger role for esma as a central supervisor of the dlt pilot regime, but that turned out to be a no-go for the council negotiators. let me, to finish, express my thanks to all involved in the file: the shadow rapporteurs, particularly jessica polfjärd, eva kaili, stephanie yon-courtin and ernest urtasun; the slovenian presidency; the european commission, particularly commissioner mcguinness; and of course the parliamentary services.
member of the commission. – madam president, i would just like to say how pleased i am, actually, to be here this evening for this discussion on what has been a very successful agreement on the distributed ledger technology (dlt) pilot project. and i want to begin by thanking, of course, the rapporteur from the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ), mr van overtveldt, who has given an extremely good rundown of what has been achieved and indeed given us some warnings about the future, which i have taken good note of. but together we’re paving the way for the future of european finance with this initiative. digital innovation is becoming ever more important, and again, i am pleased that this house shares the commitment of the commission to support innovation within the regulated space. as you know, this regulation forms a key part of our digital agenda and ensures that eu financial services legislation is fit for the digital age. we are providing regulatory certainty to pioneer the use of distributed ledger technology in financial services. so briefly, i want to mention some of the key elements of the agreed regulation. the digital representation of financial instruments on distributed ledgers is expected to open opportunities for efficiency improvements in trading and post-trading processes. the issuance of traditional asset classes in tokenised form will enable them to be issued, stored and transferred on a distributed ledger, which will also increase efficiency. with this regulation, the european union is opening up to the use of dlt in existing financial market infrastructures such as investment firms, market operators and central securities depositories. in concrete terms, it will allow for targeted exemptions from existing rules where those rules would pose technical obstacles to achieving the full benefit of dlt. as regards supervision, national competent authorities will remain in charge for authorisation, while the european securities and markets authorities (esma) can issue an opinion on application. an explanation would be needed if national competent authorities decide to deviate significantly. and last but not least, consumer protection. dlt operators will have to put in place mechanisms to handle client complaints and compensation. in the trilogues, i know that it was especially important for parliament to ensure a level playing field, and a significant role for esma. i’m glad that we were able to find good solutions and significantly improve the commission proposal in these areas. the pilot regime is, by its nature, temporary. it will last five years. this will allow market participants to safely experiment with the dlt to issue, trade and settle securities. this experience will inform future policymaking in digital finance and could open the door to more policy initiatives. before i finish, i want to thank again the rapporteur and all of the shadow rapporteurs, because this is groundbreaking work and i look forward to our debate.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, i should like to thank commissioner mcguinness for being here. i would like to start by thanking the negotiation team under the leadership of johan van overtveldt for the great cooperation on this file. by early on agreeing on several core principles, we made a good proposal even better. the dlt pilot regime is an important step in the work to make europe fit for the digital age. european rules must be innovation-friendly, and this agreement is a good example of sound european lawmaking. as we never know what the future will bring, we should allow experimenting to see what potential benefits new technology might have. and i believe dlt could indeed bring several improvements to how financial markets function. but we will, of course, not know if we don’t try. we have managed several successes in this work. first of all, legal certainty for market participants wanting to try new technologies. also expanding the scope and thresholds will make it more attractive to participants in general. and this is crucial in ensuring the usefulness of this pilot regime. if no one tries, we cannot identify the shortcomings. if we want to use this pilot regime to identify potential changes to the existing rule book, we have to ensure that enough actors participate. i believe it bodes well for future legislation as well, that both in the european parliament and in trilogues, we so easily agreed on the principle of technology neutrality. furthermore, by making use of efma’s expertise in the annual reports, both market participants and regulators will get a better understanding of the progress of functioning, which i believe will be crucial in the coming years. in many ways, this agreement is not only a good example of a european rapporteur. – madam president, legislation that is not followed or upheld is quickly something that just becomes a paper product. and this is particularly clear in the context of the european union, where theory and practice quite often differ. it simply doesn't matter how good or well-written a set of rules are if there's no compliance with them. the issue we're dealing with tonight is the revision of the schengen evaluation and monitoring mechanism, which in other words simply is the union's tools of ensuring compliance with the schengen acquis. the proposed position of parliament that we're going to vote on tomorrow can be summarised in a few points. first, the inspections carried out should, to a greater extent, be unannounced. we know that the position of council is that member states should receive a heads-up at least 24 hours before an inspection takes place. and with the current system, this is what we mean with unannounced. but frankly, it goes without saying that the best example of how a border operates is obtained by inspecting it without any prior notice. therefore, parliament's position emphasises that unannounced inspections should be given special priority, especially when international security risks are suspected. another priority is the question of the follow-ups. there must be consequences if serious shortcomings are discovered in the implementation of the schengen regulation. there must also be strict deadlines when identical flaws are to be resolved, something that is missing apparently in the current regulation. we also call for insight from the european parliament. it is reasonable that such an important common issue, like the implementation of schengen, are subject to a degree of parliamentary transparency. this report therefore proposes that parliament should have the right to send observers to return visits after previously identified shortcomings are made. we also need more inspections. our external border is never stronger than its weakest link. therefore, the commission should carry out inspections in all member states, not just the ones that happen to be the focus of attention at any given time. and the last thing that i would like to mention as this introduction is that once serious shortcomings have been identified, it is vital that council addresses the faults as common challenge for the whole union. in general, when it comes to schengen issues we need to foster a culture of mutual dialogue and trust between member states, especially when flaws in the implementation of schengen rules are detected. i am very pleased with the strong support that this report received in the committee and i would like to urge colleagues that also supported now in a plenary vote tomorrow.
the rapporteur. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i would like to conclude by thanking my colleagues from the other groups for their cooperation and support. it is my opinion that we found compromises that truly reflect parliament's general view on this mechanism. no matter how we view the schengen acquis in general, it is important that we can all agree that rules are to be followed and that the union must have the tools at its disposal to ensure that this is possible, even in practice. finally, i would just like to say the following: this is a consultation process. council is bound to wait for parliament's opinion, but it is not forced to adapt it. i now speak for an overwhelming majority in this house when i say that the next time the schengen monitoring and evaluation mechanism is revised, parliament is expecting that the revision will be done through an ordinary legislative procedure, in which the council will have equal standing with parliament. so with this, i will end by thanking the commissioner for being with us today, and for answering our concerns so well and agreeing on key issues with parliament.

on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, i welcome the commission’s announcement to put forward, later this year, a legislative proposal under the european green deal on the right to repair. it is an important matter, as repairing broken or damaged products can save consumers money by helping them to postpone making replacement purchases, while bringing benefits to the environment. today, we are asking for effective options for everyone who wants to repair electronics and other devices. in the resolution, we asked for lowering technical, legal, and economic barriers that make it difficult for consumers to repair their own goods. i also believe it should go together with the importance of a sound business environment. that is why my group has put forward several amendments at the committee and plenary level. we strongly believe in the business balance, respect for trade secrets and safeguards such as impact assessments. indeed, we should avoid any disproportionate costs on manufacturers and distributors. we also suggested to improve the single market, especially for services, by removing cross-border barriers for providing repairs. this was regretfully not included in the final text of the resolution. by removing unjustified cross-border barriers for repairs, we would indeed deliver measurable results for citizens, such as more available repair services and more competition. it would eventually bring affordable and better-quality repairs. i strongly believe that we should refrain from considering any measure that could impose disproportionate costs. we should keep as one of our core values the protection of manufacturers, distributors, citizens and consumers across the single market. i hope the commission will take this into account when proposing the new legislative proposal on the right to repair.
madam president, madam commissioner, redesign, reduce, reuse, repair, renew, recover and recycle. this, as you know, is the rule of the seven res, the fundamental pillar of the circular economy. it includes a fundamental step: repair. the right to redress that the european commission intends to create must be based on the circular economy, one of the cornerstones of the green deal. we therefore welcome the commission's presentation a week ago of the circular economy package of proposals, in particular the proposal for a regulation on the ecodesign of sustainable products, based on the seven-res rule i have just mentioned. in general, we have a serious problem with waste management, also in the european union. that is why we must work on proposals that fight planned obsolescence and empower citizens. it's not just an environmental issue, it's an ethical one, too. it's time to break the habit of consumption, of use and throw away. we must return to the commitment to quality and durable products that we made in the past and find ways of making this profitable for our industry and, in particular, for our smes. it should also be an attractive option for consumers, so that repairing a product is an alternative to be assessed before they are obliged to buy a new product. the circular economy will be a competitive advantage not only for our industry, but above all for the environment. let's bet on the circular economy. let's go for the right to redress.
madam chairman! one planet is not enough if the whole world lived as we do here in europe. if the whole world built houses, bought cars, clothes and electronics, as we do in europe, we would need 2.8 earths. it goes without saying that the way we live and produce and buy in europe is not sustainable. we need to get away from this take-it-and-throw-it mentality. an important step towards more sustainable production and consumption is the right to repair. i am naturally pleased that the european commission is considering various options, following pressure from us in the european parliament. there are a lot of things that are hindering us from being able to repair and consume more sustainably. it is time-consuming, it is difficult to get spare parts, and the price of spare parts is actually high too. but we have to change things. we need our products to last longer and also to be able to be repaired. so i would like to urge the commission, in its work towards a legislative proposal, not just to give us a right to information about repairs, but to ensure that we as consumers have a real legal right to have our products repaired. i really believe that consumers can be used to push this green transition forward. if we have the ability as consumers to demand that the product be repaired, then more people will do it, and if more people do it, then we really get the circular economy going. there is good business in it, and there may be a few who lose their jobs, but then there are some other jobs that come in instead. it is a very good combination, and we also know that it is something that consumers are demanding. in fact, 77 per cent of eu consumers prefer to have their products repaired rather than buy new ones. so everything indicates that there will be a lot of support for it, too. we must also, of course, do all the other things we have proposed and which i support. we need to design products to last longer. we have to make sure that the public buys in a lot more green, we have to have a product passport and all these things. but frankly, basically, it's important to us to have a right to have our products repaired.
madam president, we have all faced the frustration of a cracked phone screen, only to go to the manufacturer and be told that fixing it will cost almost as much as a new phone and, if we do it ourselves, it will be out of warranty for everything else. we need to listen to our citizens and we must change this. fixing items must be easier and local businesses and individuals should be able to do it. at the same time, we must do this in a proportionate way. just because something is fixable doesn’t always mean it’s a better product. if something is fixable but breaks every week, is that product better than something that just keeps working for years, but that, once it’s done, it’s done? sustainability doesn’t always equal reparability. we all support a future with better repair options. the days of throwaway culture must be over. however, this should not lead to higher prices for businesses and in turn for consumers. instead, we must incentivise manufacturers over time to adopt new business models, which are a win—win for consumers and for the environment.
madam president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop. ladies and gentlemen! no doubt each of us has experienced times when it was more costly to replace broken equipment than to repair it. repairs are often expensive and time-consuming, and spare parts are often difficult to obtain. as a result, europe is the third largest producer of e-waste we produced over 12 million tonnes of e-waste in 2020. moreover, it is projected that by 2030, this number could double. meanwhile, three quarters of europeans would rather repair broken equipment than replace it. an effective right to repair has enormous potential both environmentally by reducing waste and economically by increasing the competitiveness of businesses and stimulating innovation or job creation. this is also a significant benefit for the consumer, who should have a choice as to whether and how to repair or replace faulty equipment. however, i would like to stress that a thorough cost-benefit analysis is necessary to ensure that the proposed actions are properly balanced and take into account a number of factors such as the cost of repairs and spare parts, adequate access to repairs or the burden on smes.
madam president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop. that's not good news. i have three juicers in my house because two of them have some minor glitches that either can't be fixed or aren't worth it. we're forced to buy new equipment all the time. the average european produces almost 16 kilograms of e-waste per year, and we have long been demanding a well-functioning right to repair. and what is this right to repair? this is, on the one hand, the obligation to produce equipment in such a way that repairs are possible and cost-effective for the consumer and that spare parts are available for a reasonable period of time on the market, and, on the other hand, information for the consumer that he understands, with clear instructions that indicate the possibilities of using the many repair points. and consumers, when they choose a product in a store, should also be able to compare not only the price and quality of the product, but also the possibility of repairing the product. so i am very pleased that the european commission has responded so positively to our appeals and is taking concrete action to remedy the situation. thank you so much for that. i look forward to a fruitful collaboration. thank you, commissioner.
president, commissioner, many people tell me that their aircraft are running out of time. that sounds like a gut feeling, but no, it's true. on average, appliances break down 20% faster than they did 20 years ago. this is a direct attack on the environment and on people's wallets, who have to buy new copies all the time. it is no wonder that eight out of ten europeans want manufacturers to make their products easier to repair. we have understood that very well here in the european parliament. this resolution calls for the user to be the centre of attention. it deserves a non-binding, but enforceable right to redress, and it must be given access to the correct information. that way, we make repairs affordable, we create a lot of new, locally anchored jobs, and our products really last longer.
madam president, i would like to thank you. if europe is to succeed in meeting its climate goals, we must move from a 'use and throw' society to a more circular economy with sustainable growth. electronic waste is the fastest growing waste stream in the world. it's a huge waste of resources and an environmental disaster that the world cannot afford. but there is hope. today, more and more companies, both large and small, are working to develop new sustainable products and services with growth as their driver, with repair and rental services becoming an increasingly important part of this. using resources more efficiently and extracting more value from what is produced is good for the climate and it is good for entrepreneurship and creates more jobs. but to take every opportunity to want to stop growth and shame those who consume will rather stifle than help the climate. prohibitions, taxes and new bureaucracies are not the answer, but we must break down barriers to trade and remove tariffs on low-carbon goods and open up the market for services and rental, remove the bureaucracy of recycling and clear the labelling jungle so that consumers can trust that what they are buying is truly sustainable. so let's make the right-to-repair law a success in climate change and not another example of political greenwashing.

rapporteur. – mr president, we’ve seen it before with the global pandemic, and all over again now: education is key during crisis and wars. this report gives emphasis to the importance of education for children’s well-being as a fundamental right, enshrined in the un convention on the rights of the child. and the report comes at a very timely moment, with the horrible war raging in ukraine, weighing a huge toll on children, forcibly displacing them with 4.5 million who left the country and 7 million internally displaced within ukraine itself. for the millions of ukrainian children affected by the war, the distance learning programmes enacted during the global pandemic have proven life-saving and fundamental in ensuring the continuation of these essential formative years of children and young people’s development. as it has been for ukraine, so it is for all children in europe and around the world; education cannot wait and is never negotiable. all over the world, children and young people are experiencing new heights of mental health issues. as legislators, we are called upon to make choices and provide answers because we risk a lost generation if we do not intervene with corrective measures aimed at mitigating these very consequences. we cannot allow ourselves to fail the children in our societies all over the world. we need to do our best to ensure a bright future for the younger generation and make sure that they thrive and reach their full potential, wherever they are, holding no one back but also leaving no one behind. education is one of the most important challenges of the century, and it needs to be treated as a matter of priority, especially in these times of crisis. schools and education provide a safe space for many children around the world, and that’s even truer for girls, giving them a healthy meal a day, shielding them from domestic violence or, even worse, from being sexually abused or exploited or from being recruited as soldiers. so let’s also think about the huge economic losses associated with a lack of proper education – both formal and vocational. children who do not go to schools miss out on important skills that will be necessary for them to enter the labour market when they reach adulthood. with my report, we make an important pledge and give specific recommendations to the eu member states, the commission and the external action service to prioritise education in the recovery plans and in their relationships with third countries. education serves as a catalyst for innovation and human development, including easing young people’s access to the labour market. so this report draws a clear connection between education and entry in the labour market later in life. i will always remember my own teachers who taught me to write with a pen in my mouth, and how she forced me to always try again when i didn’t do get good enough, even if it took me 15 minutes longer than all my classmates. but that helped shape me into who i am today. we know that teachers help shape the next generation, so let’s make sure that all children have a teacher to remember. i want to thank all my shadows and i hope for the full support of all my colleagues on this important topic so close to our hearts.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as an economist, i understand the importance of entrepreneurship and enterprise. as an individual, i understand the importance of being able to choose. and as a woman, i understand the importance of equal opportunity, because it's equal opportunity, not equal outcomes, that should be the self-evident principle even when it comes to business and entrepreneurship. but that is not the case here in the european parliament. here, through quotas, gender mainstreaming, targeted resources, gender perspectives and laws, they want to push for equal outcomes in all areas. what we should do instead is to invest in what benefits us all and business in general, namely education, reduced bureaucracy, access to venture capital and low taxes.

mr president, i also want to thank the rapporteur for this very comprehensive and important report. the eu enlargement process is a matter of the future of eu and trust in our union. we must therefore ensure a clear pathway for those countries aiming to join our union. when progress is made, appreciation must be shown. albania is one of those countries that have shown most progress on their commitment to european integration and democratic transformation, and have clearly shown alignment with the eu foreign policy – not least when it comes to joint actions with the eu member states after the horrific russian invasion of ukraine. it is time to advance on albania’s accession process. one way of showing the country it is on the right path is by opening the accession negotiations with that country, and to schedule the first intergovernmental conference. with this said, the albanian political parties must overcome political disputes to strengthen democracy and the economy further. every stone must also be turned to fight organised crime and corruption. but it is clear that the albanian government and citizens see the european union as their home. it is therefore time for us to open up the doors!
mr president, i would like to thank you. those who have tortured, injured and killed civilians in ukraine must be brought to justice and severely punished. sexual violence and rape are crimes that have been committed and are still being committed in ukraine. it is used, as we know, to threaten, to humiliate, to humiliate, to hurt, and we know that girls and women are the main victims. at the same time, we know that sexual violence and rape are war crimes which rarely lead to legal consequences and punishment in practice. we have to change this. we must ensure that the words we now form a majority behind also become action.
mr president, putin expected a swift victory – against all odds ukraine turned the tide. with almost 12 000 russian war crimes reported, one wonders what is emmanuel macron’s and olaf scholz’s top concern? holding war criminals to account? no. both are desperately trying to get back to business as usual – buying gas, selling arms. both want to help putin save face by pressuring ukraine to cede parts of their territory. would a french president consider ceding the french west indies to putin and his cronies to dock their yachts? would the german chancellor gift a bundesland to the russians? the french and the germans would never accept that, so why should ukrainians? dear social democrats of germany, dear liberals of france, i will be frank, your condemnation of war crimes means nothing unless you change your government’s policy of rewarding the kremlin with territorial gains from these very war crimes. this is an unjust war. it must end in a just peace. ukraine must win.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this proposal to introduce a minimum tax of 15% on companies should come from the member states and not from brussels. because the eu is not a state, nor is it sovereign, and it should have no power in tax matters. what do you think will happen when member states realise that this agreement reduces their competitiveness? or that the united states, for example, will not implement this directive? we risk a dramatic decline in european competitiveness. the member states are again being left out of the loop. it is no longer an agreement between states, but a centralised eu tax directive. an independent state can therefore no longer decide on its own tax. in sweden we are not even able to decide on such a simple matter as vat on our fuel prices. we simply cannot control our own energy mix without the help of the eu. step by step, power is being irrevocably transferred to brussels, when we should be relying on democracy and returning it to the people.

mr president, so we have a report on the climate change and defence roadmap. a report that calls on the eu to support states in developing their capabilities to tackle environmental crime, further enhance operational effectiveness by reducing the greenhouse gas emissions intensity of military missions and operations and embark on a path towards climate neutrality by 2050 and to mainstream, of course, climate and environmental issues into the ordinary military training system at tactical and strategic levels. colleagues, while we are outfitting our battleships with sails, and covering our tanks with solar panels, and replacing our rifles with green lasers powered by pixie dust – woke beams of light – we should ask ourselves, will this deter russia from future aggression?
madam president, i would like to thank you. yes, there is nothing more permanent than a temporary eu grant. the trade union has become a subsidy union, and as new crises arise, new subsidies and now debts are created for swedish taxpayers. while many swedes are crushed by skyrocketing gasoline prices and electricity bills, and while small entrepreneurs, farmers and ranchers go bankrupt, their tax money goes to countries that have learned to live on eu subsidies instead of reforming themselves. for example, trevi nano, a small picturesque village in italy with 142 inhabitants, now receives, like 20 other picturesque villages, 200 million crowns in coronavirus aid: 1.4 million per inhabitant. what a jackpot! how is that even possible, you may ask? it is, thanks to magdalena andersson and her social democratic government, which, without blinking, has repeatedly said yes to unconditional contributions of various kinds, in solidarity, not with the swedish people but with countries which refuse to reform.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development. – mr president, colleagues, what kind of a world we want to leave behind for the future generation through our actions or inactions says a lot about what kind of humanity that exists today. the carbon border adjustment mechanism is one of the most important tools for us in the eu to reduce gas emissions in non-eu countries. the one—billion—dollar question is how to spend the estimated eur 1 billion per year in cbam revenues 2026 to 2030. we can decide to continue as business as usual through adding it to the next generation eu loans, or we can try to be revolutionary through investing it in the next generation eu and global citizen through new climate protection measures in developing countries. the reinvestment of revenues in climate resilience would set a precedent for the future cbam systems. it is in the interest of our planet, vulnerable communities and the eu that revenues from such systems are used for climate actions rather than for continued public expenditures reductions. it is in the interest of us all. we need a just transition guided by solidarity that sees beyond national borders because we are not isolated islands.
madam president, when witnessing the climate debate, it sometimes feels a little bit like an auction. the highest bidder is regarded as the winner. but i believe that there are actors bidding without actually caring if they can cover the costs. as negotiator of the ets, i have seen this behaviour as well, with the greens wanting to remove protection from carbon leakage as early as possible, not taking into account all the pathways looks like. and you can’t just tap your finger and get new carbon—free industry in place over a day. it has to be approved, constructed and for some sectors we still need innovation to get where we want. we can’t control this by our whims because we are politicians, not magicians. our industries in europe are on board with the green deal. we should be proud of how they invest and innovate to show that we can combine competitiveness with the green deal. we need to lead them along this path, to reward the best performers, to incentivise those lagging behind, while protecting them from unfair competition from china and india. the outcome from the environment committee, unfortunately, does not do that. it’s just going to hurt european competitiveness, threaten our jobs and risk increased emissions outside of europe. i therefore urge members to support the outcome on this from the industry committee instead.
the commission has also adopted a communication on the implementation of the european year of the environment (2007-2013). i'm not going to let you do this. you and the centre party's policies are making the swedes poorer and poorer. we're seeing skyrocketing electricity and energy prices, an inflation we haven't seen in many, many years. and yet you just keep going. now you also want the swedes to pay for other countries' skyrocketing energy and electricity prices through the social fund. i wonder, is there any limit to your generosity with swedish tax dollars and your naive climate dreams?
madam president, the great political project of the european union today, and for the years to come, is called the green deal. a great deal to transform our destructive relationship with the environment and natural resources and curb climate change. this is a big transformation that requires the effort of everyone and offsets like the social climate fund so that no one is left behind. it's an unstoppable transformation, you know? the scientific evidence for the climate crisis and the examples we see every day are staggering. in this situation, it would be a grave mistake to lower the ambition of the objective 55 package. we cannot give ourselves more time because we do not have this time. the current context of economic uncertainty cannot be an excuse for continuing to delay our commitment to the next generations to the point of utter irresponsibility. a just green transformation, based on innovation and technology, is not only the only way to tackle the climate and energy crisis, but also an opportunity to modernise and strengthen our economies. we have to live in the moment.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner timmermans, in order to achieve concrete environmental objectives, it is essential to create the conditions for important sectors of european industry, which are very exposed to international competition, to remain competitive throughout the transition phase and thus be able to make the necessary investments, avoiding that their production is replaced by production outside the eu, which does not meet our environmental and social standards at all. pollution does not stop at borders. cbam and ets will have a significant impact on industry and citizens. we have been working on these two dossiers in an unjustifiable hurry, without taking the slightest account of the changed economic, geopolitical and energy conditions, which are not even remotely comparable to those existing when the european commission drew up the proposals. the proposals that parliament has so far only succeeded in making worse, by competing for the most media-friendly climate objective, by following a logic completely detached from reality, by protecting only certain special interests at the expense of the real interests of the vast majority of our citizens and our businesses. the ideological slogan of saving the world risks irreparably damaging the competitiveness of key sectors of european industry without any overall environmental benefits. i have not met a single entrepreneur who has not shown himself to be more than supportive of environmental protection. our companies need to be supported in the transition, not blamed and punished, bound to achieve totally unrealistic goals and burdened with increasing costs. where do you think these increases will fall if not on the weaker bands? we've already been hit hard by the pandemic. the war in ukraine is having devastating effects on countless human and economic levels. let's carefully reassess the entire climate legislative package before we do irreparable damage to our society without...
